Retain only passage-supported, materially useful evidence; correct summaries only by narrowing; preserve disagreement and state when the future judge should reopen the full ledger. Do not score and do not invent IDs. Return only schema JSON.

GUIDE:
# 8B. Ruler Effectiveness and Competence

Status: draft; v4 objective-evidence questions require controlled rejudgment

## Chapter Identity

- Chapter: `8B`
- Category: `ruler_effectiveness_and_competence`
- Evidence strategy: one ruler-period dossier, researched across ten overlapping lenses
- Judge strategy: one chapter judge applies one comparative meter across all eligible rulers
- Rubric version: `chapter_8b_v4`

## Chapter Purpose

Assess how effectively the ruler defines a governing program, converts it into
state or ruling-network action, coordinates implementation, adapts, and advances
their own stated or reliably revealed objectives. The ten questions are evidence
lenses feeding one final 1-10 chapter score, not equal sub-scores.

This chapter is ideology-neutral. Moral worth and execution competence are
separate. A ruler may execute a cruel, aggressive, corrupt, or authoritarian
program competently and score highly in 8B while scoring very poorly in other
chapters. Never smuggle moral approval or condemnation into the competence score.

## Ten Evidence Lenses

| Lens | Simple question | Detailed research question | Priority evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| **8B.1 — Clear governing program** | Were the ruler's real goals clear enough to evaluate? | Did the ruler state or reliably reveal a sufficiently clear program in dated speeches, manifestos, strategies, directives, or formal acts to freeze and test its policy, ideological, power, and international goals? | **Rhetoric and representations**; **Formal acts and law** |
| **8B.2 — Turning goals into machinery** | Did the ruler turn goals into concrete plans, rules, resources, people, and institutions? | Did the ruler translate that program into enacted laws, budgets, appointments, timelines, institutions, regulations, and enforcement mechanisms within actual authority? | **Formal acts and law**; **Personnel**; **Implementation and operational conduct** |
| **8B.3 — Mobilizing the ruling system** | Did the ruler effectively mobilize the state and ruling network toward those goals? | Did executed resources and administrative records show effective mobilization of the state, party, military, coalition, or ruling network toward the ruler's chosen program? | **Resources**; **Personnel**; **Implementation and operational conduct** |
| **8B.4 — Capable implementers** | Did the ruler choose and manage people capable of carrying out the program? | Did the ruler appoint, empower, retain, and when necessary replace people capable of executing the program, whether professionals, technocrats, organizers, loyal operators, or coercive administrators? | **Personnel**; **Implementation and operational conduct**; **Outcomes** |
| **8B.5 — Coordination and control** | Did the ruler maintain coordination and follow-through across the system? | Did the ruler maintain documented coordination, territorial reach, milestone completion, and compliance across ministries, regions, institutions, security forces, and implementing agencies? | **Implementation and operational conduct**; **Outcomes** |
| **8B.6 — From plans to practice** | Did the ruler turn plans and announcements into real government action? | Did legislation, budgets, and directives become observable enforcement, services, projects, and institutional practice rather than remain slogans, plans, or symbolic acts? | **Formal acts and law**; **Implementation and operational conduct**; **Outcomes** |
| **8B.7 — Progress toward chosen goals** | Did results move toward the ruler's own goals? | Did outcome and distribution indicators move toward the ruler's frozen goals after accounting for baseline, realistic lag, authority, external shocks, and plausible causal alternatives? | **Outcomes**; **Implementation and operational conduct** |
| **8B.8 — Learning and correction** | Did the ruler adapt and correct course when methods failed? | Did audits, evaluations, and implementation failures lead the ruler to adapt methods, replace implementers, reallocate resources, and correct course? | **Personnel**; **Implementation and operational conduct**; **Outcomes** |
| **8B.9 — Managing crises and resistance** | Did the ruler handle crises and resistance without losing the chosen program? | Did formal decisions and implemented responses to crises, opposition, international relations, and institutional resistance preserve or advance the ruler's chosen objectives and durable control? | **Formal acts and law**; **Implementation and operational conduct**; **Outcomes** |
| **8B.10 — Durable goal achievement** | By the end, had the ruler achieved more of the program in a lasting way? | By the end of the period, had the ruler converted more of the frozen program into durable law, institutions, capacity, state practice, and achieved outcomes than at the start, accounting for failures and long-term fragility? | **Outcomes**; **Formal acts and law**; **Implementation and operational conduct** |

Priority evidence categories indicate promising evidence, not required coverage or an
exclusive source list. Missing categories do not invalidate a lens.

## Scope and Non-Goals

- Establish an explicit or reliably revealed program before judging execution.
  Repeated discriminating choices may reveal goals; hindsight speculation may not.
- Evaluate strategic clarity, operationalization, mobilization, personnel fit,
  coordination, implementation, outcomes, adaptation, crisis management, and
  durable progress as mutually informing evidence.
- Do not judge whether goals are democratic, peaceful, lawful, prosperous, humane,
  or personally honest. Those judgments belong in 1B-7B.
- Do not reward repression, propaganda, longevity, survival, economic growth,
  administrative size, or institutional quality by itself. Ask whether it
  effectively served the ruler's identified program.
- Do not assume failure from an outcome the ruler did not seek, except where
  maintaining basic government function was necessarily part of governing.
- Do not use the client matrix as evidence.

Before reading outcomes or assigning a score, freeze a portfolio of the ruler's major
stated or reliably revealed programs plus necessary core-government functions. Apply
the same breadth rule across regime types. Do not cherry-pick the easiest achieved
goal, reduce a democratic government to its broadest welfare promises, or reduce an
authoritarian government to survival, weapons, or power consolidation. Survival and
consolidation may be one material program component, never the entire portfolio by
default.

## Researcher Workflow and Evidence Themes

Follow the local-first researcher guide and source-confidence registry. Query the
exact case locally. WGI, V-Dem, BTI, budgets, and outcome indicators are useful
context or corroboration, but generic state capacity is not ruler effectiveness.

Research chronologically across themes rather than issuing one search per lens:

1. Identify stated programs from platforms, manifestos, coalition agreements,
   speeches, strategies, directives, budgets, and plans. Identify revealed goals
   only from repeated choices that distinguish them from rhetoric or speculation.
2. Trace conversion into priorities, resources, laws, institutions, appointments,
   timelines, enforcement, and measurable milestones.
3. Examine whether the state, party, military, coalition, bureaucracy, territorial
   network, or informal machinery actually mobilized, coordinated, and followed
   through; include bottlenecks and implementation gaps.
4. Assess whether personnel were capable for the assigned program. Ideological or
   coercive operators may be effective implementers in this narrow chapter even
   when their conduct is reprehensible elsewhere.
5. Compare promised/revealed objectives with implementation and outcomes after
   realistic lags, inherited trends, peer conditions, shocks, and resources.
6. Trace learning, replacement, reallocation, crisis response, resistance
   management, short-term gains, reversals, durability, and end-of-period position.

Prefer primary program and budget documents, legislation, administrative and
audit records, implementation data, credible outcome series, contemporaneous
records, serious academic histories, independent evaluations, and reputable
reporting. Official sources establish declared goals and formal actions but not
successful execution by themselves. Preserve failures, contradictions, abandoned
priorities, propaganda-only claims, alternative causal explanations, and adverse
side effects; record the latter for cross-chapter use without morally deducting
them in 8B.

Reuse each stable evidence item across relevant lenses. Natural-language coverage
and gaps are acceptable; publication must not depend on exact status terms,
sequential IDs, or exhaustive mapping syntax.

## Common Evidence-Item Semantics

Each material evidence item should carry a stable evidence ID; a concise factual
claim; source URL, title, publisher, and publication/access date where available;
source type and confidence with a short reason; a quotation or precise locator
when available; target-year/ruler-period fit; ruler attribution and
inherited-baseline relevance; its positive, negative, mixed, contextual, or
contrary role; and the supported lens IDs. Fields may be normalized later and
natural-language equivalents are acceptable. The substantive minimum is a
traceable source, intelligible claim, temporal fit, attribution, and evidence
role. One item may support multiple lenses without duplication.

## Attribution, Sparse Evidence, and Historical Cases

Separate ruler capability from inherited state capacity, predecessor programs,
subordinate initiative, favorable trends, external patrons, and luck. Assess
formal/informal control, tenure, coalition and institutional constraints,
resources, shocks, lag, ownership of appointments and priorities, response to
implementation information, and plausible counterfactual trajectory.

For obscure or historical rulers, later scholarship, archives, diplomatic
records, budgets, institutional histories, and observable implementation may be
more informative than missing modern indicators. Oral or informal programs can
be evidenced by repeated choices. Sparse evidence should reduce confidence and
widen the defensible range, not force a neutral score or invalidate the case.
Short tenure may permit judgment of direction and mobilization while leaving
outcomes unresolved. Closed systems may document plans abundantly while hiding
failures; official volume is not proof of execution.

## Holistic 1-10 Chapter Rubric

Synthesize the causal chain from program through action to durable progress.
Lenses are redundant and qualitatively weighted: clear plans with no execution
cannot score highly; favorable outcomes without ruler-attributable direction or
action warrant limited credit; a major strategic collapse can outweigh several
administrative successes. Never calculate a simple mean or score by citation
count.

Strategic clarity, centralized control, personnel discipline, and planning without
independently evidenced implementation and goal progress cannot exceed 5. Scores above
5 require attributable implementation plus progress across material parts of the
frozen portfolio. Scores above 7 additionally require independent evidence of outcomes,
durability, and adaptation rather than official architecture, coercion, longevity, or
survival alone. Harmful programs remain morally neutral here, but they must still show
actual advancement rather than mere capacity to command.

| Score | Chapter anchor |
|---:|---|
| 1 | No coherent program or effective governing control, or catastrophic inability to mobilize and execute: recurring chaos, paralysis, collapse, or decisive movement away from the ruler's own objectives. |
| 2-3 | Weak or unstable direction and serious operational failure; plans remain largely symbolic, coordination repeatedly breaks down, adaptation is poor, and major objectives substantially recede. |
| 4-5 | Mixed competence: some clear priorities and real delivery coexist with major implementation gaps, inconsistent control, failed initiatives, weak adaptation, or fragile/temporary results. |
| 6-7 | Generally effective direction and execution across important priorities, with meaningful mobilization and progress but notable delays, failures, constraints, reversals, or durability concerns. |
| 8-9 | Strong strategic-operational alignment, capable personnel and coordination, adaptive implementation, and substantial durable advancement of the ruler's program across major priorities. |
| 10 | Exceptional, sustained mastery of direction, mobilization, execution, adaptation, crisis/resistance management, and durable goal realization under demanding conditions. |

These anchors apply equally to benevolent and harmful programs. The rationale
must separately name the program and state that moral effects are evaluated in
other chapters.

## Confidence

Report confidence separately on a 0-100 scale. Consider program identification,
ruler attribution, causal trace from inputs to implementation/outcomes, source
independence, time fit and lag, external-shock controls, source/theme diversity,
closed-system opacity, and contrary explanations. Missing lenses lower confidence
rather than automatically lowering the chapter score. Give a defensible score
range where evidence or attribution is materially uncertain.

## One-Judge Comparative Workflow

One judge owns 8B across all eligible rulers for the target year/period:

1. Read every 8B dossier, identify each ruler's explicit or reliably revealed
   program, and quarantine identity failures—not sparse evidence.
2. Separate moral evaluation from execution before scoring. Record cross-chapter
   harms without deducting them here.
3. Establish batch anchors spanning democratic and authoritarian, beneficial and
   harmful, modern and historical, high- and low-capacity contexts.
4. Assign tentative holistic scores along the program-to-results chain, then
   compare neighbors for consistent treatment of inherited capacity, resources,
   shocks, tenure, lag, luck, resistance, and durability.
5. Revisit cases dominated by generic governance indicators, official claims,
   survival/tenure, favorable outcomes without attribution, or moral halo/horns.
6. Emit one chapter score, confidence/range, identified program, rationale,
   strongest execution and failure evidence, unresolved causal alternatives,
   missing themes, and review flag.

## Common Chapter-Judge Output Envelope

The judge returns one object per ruler using these exact semantic field names so
all eight chapter outputs can be normalized by the same inexpensive formatter:

- `chapter_id`: `8B`
- `rubric_version`: `chapter_8b_v4`
- `calibration_batch_id` and `calibrated_against`
- `score_1_to_10`, or null with `insufficient_evidence_reason`
- `confidence_score` and `plausible_score_range`
- `decisive_positive_evidence` and `decisive_negative_evidence`, referencing
  stable evidence IDs
- `inherited_baseline_and_constraints` and `ruler_attribution`
- `supported_lenses` and `missing_or_weak_lenses`
- `contrary_evidence`
- `source_mix` and `structured_prior_summary`
- `chapter_rationale`, `lower_anchor_rejected`, and `higher_anchor_rejected`
- `manual_review_required`, `manual_review_reason_type`, and `manual_review_reason`

Harmless serialization differences can be repaired after judgment, but the
semantic envelope must be complete. A null score is reserved for evidence too
thin or untraceable to support any responsible range, unresolved identity, or
attribution too uncertain to judge—not merely missing lenses or flexible coverage
wording. The rationale must identify the program and state explicitly that its
moral worth did not affect the execution score.

## Chapter-Specific Calibration

Use common calibration and bias checks where meaningful, plus:

- `rubric_version`: `chapter_8b_v4`
- `program_basis`: `explicit`, `mostly_explicit`, `mixed`, `mostly_revealed`,
  `speculative`, or `unclear`
- `program_type`: list from `policy`, `state_building`, `regime_control`,
  `power_consolidation`, `international_influence`, `survival`, or `other`
- `strategic_clarity`: `none`, `weak`, `partial`, `clear`, `exceptional`, or `unclear`
- `operationalization`: `absent`, `weak`, `mixed`, `strong`, `exceptional`, or `unclear`
- `mobilization_and_coordination`: `failed`, `weak`, `mixed`, `strong`,
  `exceptional`, or `unclear`
- `adaptation`: `counterproductive`, `rigid`, `mixed`, `effective`,
  `exceptional`, `not_observed`, or `unclear`
- `goal_progress`: `major_reversal`, `net_regression`, `mixed`, `substantial`,
  `exceptional`, or `too_early_or_unclear`
- `durability`: `collapsed`, `fragile`, `mixed`, `durable`, `highly_durable`,
  `too_early`, or `unclear`
- `ruler_attribution`: concise assessment of ownership, authority, inherited
  capacity, subordinate action, shocks, luck, and alternative causes
- `morality_separation_check`: explicit statement that approval/disapproval of
  objectives did not alter the score
- `lens_strengths`, `lens_gaps`, and `defensible_score_range`

Explicitly check state-capacity inheritance, survivor bias, outcome/luck bias,
propaganda and closed-system opacity, documentation advantages, hindsight-defined
goals, short-tenure/lag bias, moral halo/horns, and whether coercion was mistaken
for competence without evidence of goal advancement.

## Smoke-Test Set

These are calibration roles, not predetermined scores.

| Ruler | Country | Period | Calibration purpose |
|---|---|---|---|
| Lee Kuan Yew | Singapore | 1965-1990 | long-horizon direction, state capacity, durability |
| Deng Xiaoping | China | 1978-1992 | revealed/pragmatic program and adaptation |
| Adolf Hitler | Germany | 1933-1945 | harmful program, early execution, strategic catastrophe |
| Joseph Stalin | Soviet Union | 1928-1953 | coercive implementation and moral-separation stress test |
| Margaret Thatcher | United Kingdom | 1979-1990 | explicit democratic program, resistance, and durable change |
| Mikhail Gorbachev | Soviet Union | 1985-1991 | adaptation, partial achievement, loss of control, disputed goals |
| Boris Yeltsin | Russia | 1991-1999 | crisis, weak coordination, inherited collapse, mixed reforms |
| Paul Kagame | Rwanda | 2000-2020 | development and regime-control goals with attribution concerns |
| Volodymyr Zelenskyy | Ukraine | 2019-2023 | program transformed by extreme external shock |
| Liz Truss | United Kingdom | 2022 | short tenure, explicit program, rapid implementation failure |
| Franklin D. Roosevelt | United States | 1933-1945 | institutional mobilization across depression and war |
| Mobutu Sese Seko | Zaire | 1965-1997 | survival/personal-rule effectiveness versus state deterioration |
| Ellen Johnson Sirleaf | Liberia | 2006-2018 | post-conflict rebuilding, donor attribution, and woman ruler |
| Sirimavo Bandaranaike | Sri Lanka | 1960-1965 | historical woman ruler, coalition/state capacity, and non-Western evidence |
| Sylvanus Olympio | Togo | 1960-1963 | short, lower-visibility postcolonial program and sparse archives |

## Acceptance Checklist

- Missing implementation evidence never establishes ineffectiveness; it produces a
  null when no ruler-attributed execution fact remains. Scores above `5` require
  observable implementation or coordination, and scores above `7` additionally
  require independent evidence of attributable outcomes, adaptation, or durable
  progress across more than one material program component.

- All ten authoritative lenses are present verbatim and serve one holistic score.
- Ideology-neutrality is explicit and moral worth is separated from execution in
  research, scoring, calibration, and rationale.
- An explicit or reliably revealed program is established before execution is
  judged; hindsight speculation is labeled and constrained.
- Local-first order, citations, source profiling, and run profile are retained.
- Evidence items satisfy the common traceability, temporal-fit, attribution,
  evidence-role, and lens-link semantics.
- Ruler action is separated from inherited capacity, subordinates, luck, trends,
  shocks, tenure, and realistic outcome lags.
- Missing lenses reduce confidence rather than invalidate or mechanically depress
  the score.
- One judge compares all rulers using one meter; lenses are neither equally
  weighted nor mechanically averaged.
- Final output uses the common chapter-judge envelope, including adjacent-anchor
  comparisons and an explicit manual-review flag.
- Smoke testing and revision occur before production activation.

The changes would also reduce sanctions for candidates who fail to report campaign spending.\nLópez Obrador denies the reforms are a threat to democracy and says criticism is elitist. He argues that the funds should be redirected to helping the poor.\nRiffing on the protesters’ slogan “Don’t touch the INE (National Electoral Institute),” López Obrador said their slogans were “Don’t touch corruption,” “Don’t touch privileges,” “Don’t touch the Narco Government.”\nRelated Stories\nMexico asks US state attorneys general to investigate migrant deaths in ICE custody\n2 MIN READ\n25\nMexico to request criminal charges over deaths following fatal shooting of Houston man by ICE agents\n2 MIN READ\n\n60\nCanada calls for US and Mexico free trade agreement to be renewed for 16 years\n3 MIN READ\n“They don’t care about democracy, what they want is to continue with the oligarchy, the rule of the rich,” the president said.\nRead More\nDemonstrators say the electoral law changes approved last week threaten democracy and could mark a return to past practices of vote manipulation. Few at Sunday’s demonstration had any kind words for López Obrador, either.\n“The path he is taking is toward socialism, communism,” said Fernando Gutierrez, 55, a small businessman. “That’s obvious, from the aid going to Cuba,” Gutierrez said. López Obrador has imported coronavirus vaccines, medical workers and stone railway ballast from Cuba, but has shown little taste for socialist policies at home.\nSunday’s demonstrators were clad mostly in white and pink — the color of the National Electoral Institute — and shouted slogans like “Don’t Touch my Vote!” Like a similar but somewhat larger protest on Nov. 13, the demonstrators appeared somewhat more affluent than those at the average demonstration.\nThe heated nature of the debate drew attention from the U.S. government.\n“Today, in Mexico, we see a great debate on electoral reforms that are testing the independence of electoral and judicial institutions,” Brian A. Nichols, the U.S. assistant secretary of state for Western hemisphere affairs, wrote in his Twitter account. “The United States supports independent, well-resourced electoral institutions that strengthen democratic processes and the rule of law.”\nLópez Obrador said last Thursday that he’ll sign the changes into law, even though he expects court challenges. Many at Sunday’s protest expressed hope that Mexico’s Supreme Court would overturn some of the changes.\nLorenzo Cordova, the head of the National Electoral Institute, has said the reforms “seek to cut thousands of people who work every day to guarantee trustworthy elections, something that will of course pose a risk for future elections.”\nThe president has pushed back against the judiciary, as well as regulatory and oversight agencies, raising fears among some that he is seeking to reinstitute the practices of the old PRI party, which bent the rules to retain Mexico’s presidency for 70 years until its defeat in the 2000 elections.\nTyler Mattiace, who researches the Americas for Human Rights Watch, said it was “disappointing” that López Obrador decided to make major changes at the one part of Mexican democracy that is clearly working.\nVote counts have become much more reliable since the national electoral institute was founded in the 1990s, and the agency certified López Obrador’s own victory in 2018 elections.\n“It is worrisome that all this comes just before the 2024 elections, in a context in which the president has shown very little tolerance for those who don’t agree with him,” said Mattiace.\nElections in Mexico are expensive by international standards, in part because almost all legal campaign financing is, by law, supplied by the government. The electoral institute also issues the secure voter ID cards that are the most commonly accepted form of identification in Mexico, and oversees balloting in the remote and often dangerous corners of the country.\nLópez Obrador remains highly popular in Mexico, with approval ratings of around 60%. While he cannot run for reelection, his Morena party is favored in next year’s national elections and the opposition is in disarray.\nPart of his popular appeal comes from railing against high-paid government bureaucrats, and he has been angered by the fact that some top electoral officials are paid more than the president. But López Obrador has also openly criticized oversight and regulatory agencies, the courts and congress.\nThe opposition, tarnished by corruption scandals, has struggled to compete with the president’s popular spending and handout programs.\nRubén Salazar, the director of the Etellekt Consultores firm, said there is “a lack of leadership in the opposition to mount a defense of all these institutions like the INE and the Supreme Court.”\nFABIOLA SÁNCHEZ\nSánchez writes in Spanish about Mexico for The Associated Press, based in Mexico City. Las declaraciones de militares del 27 batallón, tanto mandos como\nmiembros de servicios de inteligencia, han ido cambiando de versión según\nse han desarrollado las investigaciones. Ocultaron inicialmente que acudieron\na barandillas y, salvo en un caso, también ocultaron que fueron al palacio\nmunicipal en la noche. A pesar de tener la evidencia de disparos y ataque a\nlos jóvenes, señalan que no salieron del cuartel bajo el argumento que no les\ncorrespondía o que tenían información de la policía municipal de que todo estaba\ncorrecto, lo cual no es cierto. Tenían la evidencia de los ataques, la información\nen tiempo real del C4, controlaban las cámaras y se transmitió en tiempo real\nel ataque a los responsables de inteligencia del 27 batallón. Inteligencia militar\ntuvo presencia en los dos lugares de ataques a los estudiantes y no solo en uno\ncomo se dijo, y tanto los OBI como sus responsables mintieron sobre ello o\nsobre las circunstancias de los hechos en varias ocasiones.\nExiste evidencia técnica de que varios mintieron sobre sus movimientos esa noche,\nsiendo mucho más amplios, estando en varios escenarios que no reconocieron\ny tiempo antes del que señalaron en sus declaraciones. Además, responsables\ndel ejército del B27 y del 41B estaban coludidos con el narcotráfico, como\npuede deducirse de las escuchas de Chicago que señalan pagos y relaciones\ncon al menos un comandante y un capitán, y mediante declaraciones de testigos\nprotegidos que han señalado como recibían dinero periódicamente para que\ndejaran llevar a cabo sus acciones a GU, lo que explica en parte su actuación,\nocultamiento y falta de protección de los jóvenes a pesar de la información\ncon que contaban esa noche. Existe evidencia contra numerosos militares cuyas\nórdenes de captura fueron canceladas por la fiscalía en septiembre de 2022,\nlas cuales tenían sustento y habían sido ordenadas por el juez, por lo que estas\ndeben reactivarse. Dichas evidencias han sido de nuevo reiteradas a la Fiscalía\nespecial UEILCA, lo que debe guiar su acción son las pruebas existentes y no\nórdenes de otra índole, tal y como se han comprometido las autoridades ante los\nfamiliares. El GIEI ha señalado prioridad de que se llevan a cabo 6 de ellas. En\nesta fecha aún no se han reactivado.\nExiste evidencia de que uno de los soldados a través de medios técnicos ve tres\ncamionetas de la policía municipal, en donde en una de ellas (la del medio)\nviajan personas de civil, la cual no se ha entregado a ningún proceso de la\nFiscalía. Lo que evidencia que se ha seguido ocultando información y mintiendo\nsobre los hechos.\n11. Señalamientos de falta de colaboración del ejército por parte de autoridades\nestatales. Deben investigarse los señalamientos de autoridades del Estado, tanto\nde gobernación del Estado como de la PGJ, realizadas ante UEILCA como ante\nel GIEI, de que se habría dado una falta de colaboración del ejército (el 27 de\n7\n\nUna visión global sobre los hechos, las responsabilidades y la situación del caso Ayotzinapa\nseptiembre de 2014) para proporcionar sus instalaciones para iniciar la custodia\nde los policías municipales que participaron en el ataque a los normalistas, que\nfinalmente se dio en las instalaciones de CRAPOL, y señalaron que no solo\npor autoridades del B27, sino por órdenes la ZM35 y por indicación directa del\ncomandante de la IX Región militar. Dicha denuncia de falta de colaboración no\nha sido investigada. Además de la información sobre colusión de las autoridades\nmunicipales que se señaló que fue compartida en el Grupo de Coordinación\nGuerrero Seguro antes de los hechos, el ejército tenía meses antes su propia\ninformación sobre la colusión del presidente municipal de Iguala, Cocula y\notras poblaciones, como ha mostrado la revisión de archivos, sin que se llevara\nninguna actuación sobre ello.\n12. El ejército tenía varios soldados infiltrados en la Escuela de Ayotzinapa.\nUno de ellos era uno de los 43 jóvenes desaparecidos. A pesar de esto, se\nocultó este hecho durante años, y después se ha señalado que se encontraba\ncon licencia para estudiar y no estaba activo, pero dicha respuesta no responde\na la verdad porque hay documentación de SEDENA que muestra que fue\nasignado con función de OBI (fachada) dentro de la Escuela y por lo tanto\ninformaba permanentemente de los movimientos de los estudiantes, asambleas\ny acciones, como otros OBI en la escuela que han seguido existiendo. De todo\nesto se encontró prueba documental que contiene información precisa de las\nactividades internas de la Escuela, de la toma de buses, de las actividades\nprevistas para el mes de septiembre de 2014. Igualmente, los OBI se reportaban\ntodos los días para dar cuenta de su situación, como ha reconocido uno de\nellos ante UEILCA.\nAdemás, existe registro en archivos de SEDENA de que un OBI se reportó\nel día 27 a sus superiores, cuando ya se habían llevado a cabo los hechos y\ndetenciones, y mencionó que no se comunicaría más por seguridad. A la petición\ndel GIEI de informar sobre esta circunstancia documentada en un FCA suscrito\npor el general de la ZM35, SEDENA ha negado tener información sobre la\nidentificación de ese soldado o sobre lo que comunicó a sus superiores, respuesta\nque para el GIEI y los familiares es inaceptable con las pruebas existentes.\nOtro OBI que cumplía funciones en la Escuela, declaró ante UEILCA que él no\nfue a la movilización porque estaba en segundo año, y eso correspondía a los\nde primer curso, lo que confirma por qué se movilizaron solo los estudiantes\nde primero. Otro tercer OBI reportó ese mismo día que vio al soldado que se\nencontraba desaparecido en la escuela el día 27, y si bien puede haber una\nconfusión sobre ello, debido a la existencia de varios OBI, a la vez muestra que\nse conocía su desaparición desde ese momento.\n8\n\nA ocho años y medio del caso\nEl comandante general de SEDENA en la época, señaló en documento oficial que\nel soldado desaparecido no se había comunicado desde el día 22 de septiembre\ny que las autoridades desconocían donde estaba, cosa que no responde a la\nrealidad. Por otra parte, desde el día 27, SEDENA tomó contacto con la familia\nde dicho OBI. Todo esto fue ocultado en la investigación durante 7 años, hasta\nque el GIEI encontró los documentos en los archivos de SEDENA tras la orden\nde acceso del presidente de México.\n13. SEDENA tenía conocimiento en tiempo real de lo que estaba sucediendo\na través de interceptaciones telefónicas cuya transcripción parcial fue\nencontrada en sus archivos en documentos. Reportes existentes en los archivos\nmilitares dan cuenta de parte de conversaciones del día 26 de septiembre entre\npersonas que decían que tenían a los muchachos y el 4 de octubre de parte de\nconversaciones, entre quienes identifican como un miembro de GU y un policía,\nsobre posible destino de los normalistas. Ninguna acción en ese momento se\ndio para proteger o rescatar a los jóvenes o buscarlos a pesar de ello. Ante dicha\nevidencia la respuesta de SEDENA ha sido que no existe esa información, que\nno se realizan interceptaciones telefónicas ni monitoreos, que desconoce los\nautores de dichas transcripciones y reportes.\nNinguna de esas respuestas se corresponde con la realidad que está en\ndocumentos del CRFI de Iguala y el DN1, EMDN y CMI. SEDENA ha negado\nla existencia de los mensajes como propios e incluso la existencia del Centro de\nFusión de Inteligencia de Iguala que llevó a cabo esas y otras acciones, a pesar\nde que numerosos mensajes y documentos que así lo acreditan.\nEl GIEI ha puesto en conocimiento de la fiscalía dichas respuestas para que se\ninvestigue a los responsables de dichas comunicaciones que ocultan la verdad\ny que muestran una respuesta negativa a las órdenes del propio presidente de\nMéxico de que se debe proporcionar toda la información existente que ayude\n9\n\nUna visión global sobre los hechos, las responsabilidades y la situación del caso Ayotzinapa\na la investigación y búsqueda de los desaparecidos. Incluso en una respuesta,\nSEDENA negó que exista documentación que pidió la UEILCA, que sin\nembargo el GIEI y la COVAJ obtuvieron de los propios archivos de SEDENA.\n14. El GIEI solicitó en varias ocasiones, tener acceso a toda la información\nrelativa a los CRFI de Iguala. Al no haber contado con ella, el propio\nPresidente ha pedido la información. Tanto el GIEI como la justicia han\ntenido información y prueba de que dichos documentos, cuya existencia se\nniega, fueron movidos y concentrados en un lugar determinado. Sin embargo,\nla respuesta de SEDENA ha sido que dicho traslado no se ha llevado a cabo,\nque dichos documentos no existen y que se debe denunciar a quienes están\ninformando de ello. "locator": "extracted HTML block 74 through extracted HTML block 76", "exact_excerpt": "By\nMARÍA VERZA\nUpdated [hour]:[minute] [AMPM] [timezone], [monthFull] [day], [year]\nAdd AP News on Google\nAdd AP News as your preferred source to see more of our stories on Google.\nShare\nShare\nFacebook\nCopy\nLink copied\nPrint\nEmail\nX\nLinkedIn\nBluesky\nFlipboard\nPinterest\nReddit\nMEXICO CITY (AP) — Mexico’s president inaugurated a huge “super pharmacy” Friday in a bid to end the woes of patients throughout the country who are often told they need a specific medicine — but the hospital in question doesn’t have it.\nPresident Andrés Manuel López Obrador’s solution was to outfit a big warehouse on the outskirts of Mexico City to centralize a supply and send it to hospitals throughout the country.\n“The pharmacy is going to be big, big, big, and it is going to have all the medications that are used in the heath system,” López Obrador said Friday.\nThe pharmacy is intended to complement local health facilities. If a patient can’t get needed medications at a local hospital, the patient, the patient’s doctor or the pharmacist would be able to call up the warehouse and get it delivered from the huge 40,000 square meter (430,000 square foot) Mexico City warehouse.\nThe armed forces, or the government-run pharmaceutical company Birmex, will ship the drugs out by land or air “within 24 to 48 hours,” López Obrador pledged.\nThe question is whether Mexico can overcome its history of being bad at regulating the pharmaceutical industry, bad at buying medicines, bad at storing them, and bad at distributing them. Extreme centralization also hasn’t helped Mexico much in the past in many areas.\nThe most visible face of this problem are the parents of children with cancer, who frequently stage protests because they say that in recent years chemotherapy and other drugs have been impossible to obtain.\n\nRelated Stories\nCuba loosens control of its private sector in a struggle with a mounting humanitarian crisis\n3 MIN READ\n13\n‘She killed the kids!’ 911 call from Lindsay Clancy’s husband brings tears to the courtroom\n4 MIN READ\n176\nFederal survey shows promising trends for youth mental health and drug use\n1 MIN READ\nDesperate parents blocked traffic at the Mexico City airport last year, holding up a banner reading: “There isn’t any chemotherapy, treatment or medicines, have some empathy and sensitivity.”\nRead More\nThe problems have killed otherwise healthy people. Because Mexico has had problems in obtaining enough morphine, anesthesiologists in Mexico have had to carry around their own vials of the sedative, drawing multiple doses out of a single vial for routine procedures like spinal blocks during births.\nIn the United States, where there is no shortage of morphine, doctors are advised to draw a single dose from a vial and throw the remainder out.\nBut in Mexico,\nthat has led to contamination of the vials,\ntriggering\noutbreaks of injection-induced meningitis in two Mexican states that have killed dozens of people\n— including some Americans who sought treatment at clinics in the border city of Matamoros, across from Brownsville, Texas.\nSign up for Morning Wire:\nOur flagship newsletter breaks down the biggest headlines of the day.\nEmail address\nSign up\nBy checking this box, you agree to AP's\nTerms of Use\nand acknowledge that AP may collect and use your data pursuant to our\n\nPrivacy Policy\n.\nLópez Obrador mounted a major effort to obtain COVID-19 vaccines in 2021, using the armed forces to distribute them and volunteers to help apply them, and by the end of that year just about anybody in Mexico who wanted a vaccine got one, for free.\nBut trying to replicate that model of centralized government purchasing and army distribution on a national scale for thousands of medications is not the same, according to Mauricio Rodríguez, a professor at the School of Medicine at Mexico’s National Autonomous University.\n“This is crazy,” said Rodríguez, noting the government is opening the centralized warehouse without answering how the system will operate, especially for urgently-needed medications. He noted that concentrating all the drugs at one site increases risks, and could sideline some already-existing distribution systems.\nMany of the problems pre-date López Obrador, who took office in late 2018. For decades, there have been scandals involving of millions of dollars worth of medicines going out-of-date at warehouses while hospitals couldn’t get them.\nThe country’s medicine regulatory agency, known by its Spanish acronym as\nCofepris, was already so riddled with corruption\nprior to López Obrador that regulators would hide applications for approval of new medicines for years, and demand bribes to approve them.\nAnd with alarming frequency, regulators in Mexico send out alerts about falsified or knock-off medications being sold for treating everything from cancer to heart disease. Boxes, labels, vials and certifications are copied with amazing accuracy, but the bottles often contain little or none of the medication.\nThe fake medicine trade is so common, and so lucrative in Mexico\nbecause patients or their relatives are often told by doctors to buy medications at private drug stores when they are unavailable at government hospitals.\nThe civic group “Zero Shortages” said there was an increase of 142% in the number of alerts about falsified medicines between 2021 and 2022.\nBut part of the problems are of López Obrador’s own making. Angry at what he claimed were inflated profits made by drug distributors and importers, the president simply cut the private companies out and decided the government should directly buy all medications.\nBecause the government did not have much infrastructure, contacts or experience in such a massive effort, López Obrador signed an agreement with the World Health Organization to help Mexico in purchasing. But even with that help, Mexico was unable to obtain some specialized medication, something López Obrador blamed on sabotage by pharmaceutical firms.\nRafael Gual, the director of the National Pharmaceutical Industry Chamber, said the government’s own actions created “bottlenecks” in distribution.\nAccording to the group “Zero Shortages,” the number of prescriptions that went unfilled in Mexico rose from 1.5 million in 2019 to 22 million in 2021; disruptions due to the COVID pandemic probably played a role.\nBut even in 2022, there were still about 12.5 million prescriptions that went unfilled.\nDr. José Moya, WHO’s Mexico representative, said centralized medical warehouses can be a solution, but the key is to have a good logistical system.\n“If they are considering a warehouse like this, it’s because there is a need,” Moya said, “and this has to be very well organized.”\nMARÍA VERZA\nVerza has focused on immigration, violence and human rights stories in Mexico and Central America for more than a decade\ntwitter\ninstagram\nmailto", "excerpt_sha256": "08c0072bdefb71e6245d121877e6cc0caa134ef36bce56093ab9e9c9bd8a816d", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The supplied excerpt does not state the inauguration date of 29 December 2023."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0013-E016", "source_id": "SRC-34635abd966dfd45", "url": "https://resolve.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/675123641D798FDC8CD528A628B3A4B2/9781009460248c10_347-379.pdf/the-rule-of-law-crisis-as-a-testing-ground-for-latin-american-constitutionalism.pdf", "title": "The Rule-of-Law Crisis as a Testing Ground for Latin American Constitutionalism", "publisher": "Cambridge University Press", "raw_sha256": "284fedeb83d11321d52c907f3d2b81b5af9cbe78f91be6b644840e976d40ea21", "fact_summary": "The chapter reports that AMLO attacked and sought to weaken the INE, proposed electoral changes that would politicize its counselors, and advanced Plan B to shrink the institute; However, under the political system\n               progressively installed by the PRI, elections were rigged and won by the\n               person designated by the president. The PRI was to a certain extent\n               inclusive of various social groups and provided a system of elite\n               rotation.61 It also provided peace and stability in a country that had\n               endured signiﬁcant turmoil during the nineteenth century.62 But it was\n               not a democracy: there were formal elections, but no actual rule-of-law\n               preconditions to ensure free voting and political competition.\n                  When it ultimately became impossible to maintain the system without\n               changes, several constitutional reforms introduced electoral competition\n               and progressively built independent electoral authorities.63 An institu-\n               tion called IFE (Instituto Federal Electoral) was created in 1990, together\n               with parallel institutions at state level. In 1996, the IFE gained complete\n               independence from the executive, and in 2013 a constitutional reform\n               renamed the institution INE (Instituto Nacional Electoral) and gave it\n               more powers over the organization and monitoring of elections at state\n               level. The institution soon established itself as one marked by profession-\n               alization (its employees are career ofﬁcials) and a model of election\n               organization and supervision that included extensive input from the\n               citizenry and was grounded in administrative rule of law. Thus, the\n               voting booths in Mexico are managed by citizens who are selected on\n               the basis of their birth month, trained before the elections by the INE, and\n               60\n                  Tom Ginsburg deﬁnes the rule of law in the administration of elections as a key compo-\n                  nent of democracy. This contained a proposal to amend six statutes\n               (something MORENA could do by a simple majority vote). At its core,\n               it involved a radical shrinking and weakening of INE as an institution, to\n               the point of compromising its ability to organize and supervise elections\n               under the rule of law.75\n                   Several parts of the project are perfectly acceptable, even to be wel-\n               comed, such as the overdue guarantee of the right for people in prison to\n               vote, the regulation of online trials, the simpliﬁcation of complaint\n               procedures, the need to implement gender parity in elections for state\n               governors, and the restructuring of the calendar for federal and state\n               elections. Yet other proposals are sensitive, and some deeply troubling. In\n               this last category are the elimination of the rule that had so far prevented\n               individuals holding public ofﬁce (and in receipt of a salary paid with\n               public money) from engaging in political advocacy in electoral cam-\n               paigns; the elimination of the INE administrative apparatus across the\n               country, endangering electoral IDs and the training of citizens who serve\n               on election day; the reorganization of the INE’s internal units, with the\n               immediate removal of the executive secretary, the dissolution of the\n               trusts guaranteeing the salaries of INE employees (which would now\n               depend on annual budgetary allocations); and the provision (tailored to\n               “save” a future Salgado Macedonio) that being sanctioned by the INE for\n               violating electoral law is no impediment to run for elections (only\n               a criminal conviction is).\n                   Plan B was published in two steps. This time, people gathered at the\n                    Zócalo, the tectonic power center in Mexico, and available estimates put\n                    the attendance in Mexico City and other cities across the country at\n                    approximately 700,000.77\n                       On March 4, 2023, however, the second package of Plan B was published.\n                    A few days later, the INE ﬁled two controversias against the reform and two\n                    groups of Congress members ﬁled acciones de inconstitucionalidad.78 As the\n                    demonstrators in the February march made clear through their banners and\n                    chants, protection of democracy and the rule of law would depend on the\n                    Supreme Court.\n                       AMLO has striven to undermine the Court using all means. He has\n                    taken advantage of the opportunity to appoint four justices, two of whom\n                    are unconditional supporters, while the other two have proven to be fairly\n                    independent. Yet, for a statute to be declared void in abstract review in\n                    Mexico, and disappear from the books, there must be a qualiﬁed majority\n                    of eight votes – a threshold difﬁcult to pass in any scenario, but specially\n                    so in the current climate. Further, for four years the presidency of the\n                    Court was held by Arturo Zaldívar, who was quickly criticized for\n                    cultivating an excessive closeness to the government. Under his presi-\n                    dency, the Court delayed decisions in many politically sensitive cases.\n                       In the electoral domain, however, the Court’s decision-making is\n                    subject to time limits. And its performance as regards Plan B has been\n                    admirably solid, even in the face of all imaginable kinds of pressure. It was in this subor-\n               dinated quality that the army was involved in the dark episodes of the\n               “dirty war” of the 1970s.83 Yet, during the gradual transition to democ-\n               racy of the 1980s and 1990s, the military was generally held in high regard\n               by the population – which would associate it with assistance during\n               natural disasters, among other tasks of that sort.\n                 The situation changed when Felipe Calderón won the presidency in\n               2006. Some two years later, although the matter had been mentioned\n               during his campaign, Calderón decided to launch a “war against drugs”\n               that put the army on the streets in several parts of the country without\n               any legal basis for doing so. Es Foto: Agencia Cuartoscuro EVALUACION%20DEL%20DESEMP.pdf", "excerpt_sha256": "ba5a5a67b61d8b20990f07f7b0b3be52a3a152bb9df53eb59594f5511df693c3", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt does not support the summary's statement that all listed bodies were 'impaired or at risk of lacking quorum'; it specifically states that Cofece lacked the quorum needed for full functions and separately discusses IFT and Inai risks."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0024-E007", "source_id": "SRC-42d976481b68a6db", "url": "https://www.cidob.org/en/lider-politico/andres-manuel-lopez-obrador", "title": "Andrés Manuel López Obrador", "publisher": "CIDOB", "raw_sha256": "bf60b32abd5810ed4b1d5de551c3063dcf90967b656bcf9bae2ec6eb09c6f71e", "fact_summary": "López Obrador’s 2018 program specified republican austerity, no new debt, gradual minimum-wage and pension increases, expanded domestic refining, review or reversal of parts of the energy reform, food and energy self-sufficiency, a National Guard with a stated goal of pacifying Mexico within three years, anti-corruption action, migrant protection, and respect for macroeconomic stability and Banco de México autonomy.", "question_ids": ["8B.1"], "polarity": "context", "period_fit": "The program was stated before the 2018–2024 presidency; it is relevant as a baseline but does not itself document 2023 results.", "ruler_attribution": "CIDOB attributes the program and commitments to López Obrador and MORENA.", "limitations": ["No supplied unit provides 2023 budget execution, institutional implementation, outcome indicators, or adaptation evidence."], "start_unit": 19, "end_unit": 20, "locator": "extracted HTML block 19 through extracted HTML block 20", "exact_excerpt": "A lo largo de 2017 López Obrador, cabeza en todas las encuestas de intención de voto, terminó de perfilar su estrategia de cara a la gran prueba electoral de 2018. En junio, durante el III Congreso Nacional del partido, y luego del reclutamiento para MORENA de los ex presidentes perredistas Leonel Godoy y Leonel Cota (a los que en enero de 2018 iba a seguir Marcelo Ebrard, fichaje estrella de la formación izquierdista), el dirigente desestimó, por una cuestión de \"valores y principios\", la oferta hecha por la presidenta del PRD, Alejandra Barrales Magdaleno, y Miguel Ángel Mancera de acudir juntos a las elecciones federales en un frente amplio de izquierda. Entonces,\nAMLO\ndescartó explícitamente cualquier alianza preelectoral con el PRD, el PRI, el PAN, el PVEM, el PANAL, el PES y el MC, por tratarse de partidos \"al servicio del régimen\", y solo aprobó una coalición con el PT.\nEn el IV Congreso Nacional Extraordinario de MORENA, el 20 de noviembre de 2017, López Obrador hizo presentación del\nProyecto Alternativo de Nación 2018-2024\n, documento base para un plan de Gobierno abierto al escrutinio y debate de simpatizantes y ciudadanos, los cuales eran invitados al aporte de diagnósticos, ideas y propuestas. Terminado de ajustar como Proyecto 18 y con Alfonso Romo Garza de coordinador general, el documento se estructuraba en cuatro comisiones temáticas, a saber, Economía y Desarrollo, Desarrollo Social, Política y Gobierno, y Educación, Valores, Cultura y Ciencia, y presentaba siete lineamientos generales, cuales eran: Legalidad y erradicación de la corrupción; Combate a la pobreza; Recuperación de la paz; Viabilidad financiera y austeridad; Equidad de género; Desarrollo sostenible y buen vivir; y Reconstrucción nacional.\nA continuación, MORENA, el PT de Alberto Anaya Gutiérrez y el PES de Hugo Éric Flores Cervantes formalizaron la coalición Juntos Haremos Historia, con López Obrador de precandidato presidencial conjunto. A pesar de lo asegurado en junio, el morenista accedió a compartir cancha electoral con un partido, Encuentro Social, habitualmente descrito como de derecha religiosa y, por cierto, la antítesis del socialista y estrictamente laico PT.\nEste entendimiento fue interpretado como una muestra del creciente pragmatismo de López Obrador y también como el reconocimiento implícito de, a título personal, una cierta proximidad ideológica al evangélico Flores en temas de fe y moralidad. El líder nacional de MORENA se definía a sí mismo como cristiano \"en la extensión amplia de lo que significa el cristianismo\" y \"seguidor de la vida y la obra de Jesucristo\". Su postura más bien conservadora en materia de costumbres sociales era sugerida por su ambigüedad o evasivas cuando se le preguntaba por su opinión sobre cuestiones tales como la despenalización del aborto, el matrimonio igualitario y la adopción homoparental.\nEl 12 de diciembre de 2017 López Obrador se registró como precandidato presidencial ante la Comisión Nacional de Elecciones de MORENA. Desde este momento, la presidencia del CEN del partido quedaba en manos de la secretaria general, Yeidckol Polevnsky. Ahora mismo, el izquierdista era el primero en las encuestas con un 30% de apoyos. El último paso oficial fue la inscripción ante el INE como candidato por la coalición tripartita Juntos Haremos Historia, el 16 de marzo de 2018. En su discurso de registro,\nAMLO\nvislumbró el advenimiento de la \"Cuarta Transformación en la historia de México\", transformación que ellos iban a lograr \"sin violencia, de manera pacífica, con información y orientación, con la difusión de principios éticos, liberando a nuestros conciudadanos del miedo y la desinformación, y llamando a todas y todos a buscar la reconciliación\".\nEl candidato se refirió también a la necesidad de un \"gobierno honesto que represente a todos\", de lograr \"el renacimiento económico, social político y, sobre todo, moral de nuestro querido México\", y de asentar \"un auténtico Estado democrático de derecho\". Ellos no compartían la máxima de \"ni perdón ni olvido\": un Gobierno de MORENA acabaría ciertamente con \"la corrupción, la impunidad y los lujos en el Gobierno\", pero los \"integrantes del poder económico y político\" del país debían de saber que \"no les guardamos rencor y les aseguramos que tras su posible derrota no habrá represalias, ni persecución, ni destierro para nadie\". \"Declaramos enfáticamente que lo que se necesita es justicia, no venganza, no odiamos a nadie\", recalcó el candidato.\nEn estos momentos, mediados de marzo de 2018, López Obrador superaba holgadamente a sus principales competidores: por la coalición oficialista Todos por México (PRI, PVEM y PANAL), el capitalino José Antonio Meade Kuribreña, uno de los pesos pesados del equipo de Peña Nieto, del que había sido sucesivamente secretario de Relaciones Exteriores, secretario de Desarrollo Social y Secretario de Hacienda y Crédito Público; y, por la coalición Por México al Frente (PAN, PRD y MC), el mexiquense Ricardo Anaya Cortés, ex diputado federal y dos veces presidente de los panistas entre 2014 y 2017. En los muestreos de intención de voto la delantera del morenista oscilaba entre los 20 y los 30 puntos. Para muchos comentaristas, esta abultada ventaja de López Obrador era irreversible, más al no existir en México el sistema de la doble vuelta.\n10. Victoria histórica en las presidenciales de 2008\nHasta las elecciones federales del 1 de julio, López Obrador, Morena y Juntos Haremos Historia fueron desgranando las claves de su programa de Gobierno.\nEl programa electoral incidía en una serie de temas.\nEn primer lugar, se abogaba por la \"austeridad republicana\", un ajuste fiscal ligado al ahorro de gastos corrientes del Estado y la reorientación de los recursos públicos a la inversión, sin subir ningún impuesto (y sí bajando algunos, pero sólo en los estados fronterizos con Estados Unidos) y sin emitir deuda. Habría un aumento gradual del salario mínimo, hasta los 171 pesos al final del sexenio, y lo mismo para las pensiones de adultos mayores, que cobrarían mensualidades de 1.500 pesos. Se incrementaría la oferta de gasolina autóctona para reducir las importaciones y rebajar los precios, lo que requería poner en servicio más refinerías. La reforma energética de Peña Nieto sería sometida a una revisión a fondo, anulando y revirtiendo cuanto fuera necesario. Igualmente, se buscaría las autosuficiencias energética y alimentaria, en el caso del agro ofreciendo a los productores locales precios de garantía.\nEn el angustioso capítulo de la inseguridad, el Estado prestaría atención a las \"causas sociales\" de la violencia, lo que pasaba por combatir la pobreza y elevar el desarrollo humano de los colectivos vulnerables. Se asumía el principio de la retirada de las Fuerzas Armadas de las misiones de seguridad interna pero sin precipitaciones, mientras se organizase y desplegase un nuevo cuerpo armado, la Guardia Nacional, con la meta de \"pacificar\" México en tres años. Por otro lado, MORENA hablaba de \"erradicar\" la corrupción política \"dando ejemplo\" institucional desde Los Pinos.\nEl candidato entonó asimismo la defensa firme de los migrantes mexicanos, más en tiempos de muro fronterizo de Trump, aranceles unilaterales a las exportaciones mexicanas de metales al vecino norteño y renegociación forzada del TLCAN, para lo que buscaría establecer con Estados Unidos unas relaciones de cooperación y buena vecindad basadas \"en el respeto mutuo\". En marzo de 2018 López Obrador y Polevnsky repitieron que MORENA quería que México dispusiera del mejor equipo de negociadores para conseguir que el tratado que sucediera al TLCAN de 1992 fuera lo más favorable posible a los intereses nacionales. Por otro lado y de nuevo, el candidato salió a desacreditar los intentos de establecer una relación simpática entre su plataforma y el régimen de Caracas: él no tenía \"nada que ver con el Gobierno de Venezuela\", y su \"mexicanismo\" equivalía a decir \"ni chavismo, ni trumpismo\", recalcó.\nLa plataforma lopezobradorista para el período de gobierno 2018-2024 sugería una socialdemocracia pragmática que ni comulgaba con la teoría de la lucha de clases del socialismo ni aspiraba a volver a los esquemas proteccionistas o estatistas del viejo PRI. Al contrario, asumía los principios ortodoxos del libre mercado, el libre comercio, la estabilidad macroeconómica, la disciplina presupuestaria, la flotación del peso y la autonomía del Banco de México, pero conjugadas, eso sí, con el rechazo al \"neoliberalismo\", las \"tendencias privatizadoras\" y la \"extranjerización\" de sectores estratégicos, pues había que priorizar la \"producción nacional\", el consumo interno y el \"crecimiento horizontal\" en todo el país. A los guiños tranquilizadores a empresarios e inversores el tabasqueño añadía la promesa de, llegada la hora de acabar con la corrupción y los privilegios de los ex presidentes, \"hacer justicia sin venganza ni odio\". \"No voy a meter a Peña Nieto en la cárcel\", aseguró también durante la campaña.\nOtras tomas de postura eran: el rechazo a un Gobierno de gran coalición, es decir, abierto a ministros del PRI o el PAN; la creación de los mecanismos legales de la revocación de los mandatos electos y el desafuero de aquellos cargos públicos procesados por corrupción; la provisión de Internet libre y gratuito en todo el territorio nacional; el cierre de las brechas de desigualdad, sobre todo en el ámbito laboral, entre hombres y mujeres; el programa de becas estudiantiles\nJóvenes Construyendo el Futuro\n; o la puesta en servicio de un Sistema Aeroportuario del Valle de México no basado en el proyecto del Nuevo Aeropuerto Internacional de México (NAIM), polémico por sus exorbitantes sobrecostes y su impacto medioambiental, sino en la habilitación del Aeropuerto Internacional de Santa Lucía (AISL) a partir de unas instalaciones militares ya existentes.\nEl 1 de abril de 2018 López Obrador, rezumando optimismo, abrió en Ciudad Juárez, Chihuahua, su tercera campaña presidencial en 12 años y el 27 de junio la cerró en un Estado Azteca, enorme cancha con capacidad para 87.000 espectadores de la Ciudad de México, lleno hasta la bandera y bañado en euforia. En las filas morenistas había ambiente de celebración por anticipado del triunfo electoral. Y no podía ser menos, a tenor de las encuestas, algunas de las cuales otorgaban a López Obrador más del 50% de los votos.\nTras cerrarse las urnas el domingo 1 de julio, el INE publicó el conteo rápido del PREP, que no dejaba dudas sobre la magnitud de la victoria, aplastante, de López Obrador. Al candidato opositor y virtual presidente electo le bastaron estos resultados preliminares para dirigir un primer mensaje desde el cuartel electoral de MORENA en el Hotel Hilton de la capital. Allí, bajo un gran rótulo que decía \"Gracias, México, no les voy a fallar\", López Obrador agradeció a sus oponentes el rápido reconocimiento de su triunfo en este \"día histórico\", llamó \"a todos los mexicanos a la reconciliación\", prometió \"erradicar\" la corrupción, \"que no es un fenómeno cultural, sino el resultado de un régimen en decadencia\", y tendió la mano a Trump, ofreciéndole \"una relación de amistad y cooperación para el desarrollo, siempre afincada en el respeto mutuo y en la defensa de nuestros paisanos que viven y trabajan honradamente en Estados Unidos\". Precisamente, Trump publicó en Twitter un cordial mensaje de felicitación y buena disposición para su flamante par mexicano.\n\nA continuación, López Obrador y su comitiva de colaboradores y familiares se trasladaron al Zócalo, donde les esperaban una muchedumbre enfervorizada, lista para escuchar el discurso de la victoria hasta bien entrada la madrugada del lunes 2 de julio. A sus seguidores,\nAMLO\nles garantizó la aplicación de \"los tres principios básicos: no mentir, no robar y no traicionar al pueblo\", anunció una \"transición con armonía\", desarrollada \"de manera ordenada, pacífica\", y prometió que los cambios iban a ser \"profundos, pero con apego al orden legal establecido\". \"Escucharemos a todos, atenderemos a todos, respetaremos a todos, pero daremos preferencia a los más humildes y olvidados; en especial, a los pueblos indígenas de México. Por el bien de todos, primero los pobres, dijo el orador, que recalcó también: \"Bajo ninguna circunstancia, el próximo presidente permitirá la corrupción ni la impunidad. Sobre aviso no hay engaño: sea quien sea, será castigado. Incluyo a compañeros de lucha, funcionarios, amigos y familiares\".\nEl 8 de julio, una vez completados los cómputos distritales, el INE publicó los resultados finales del escrutinio, que concedían la Presidencia a López Obrador con el 53,19% de los votos, habiendo alcanzado la participación el 64,4% del censo. Anaya había quedado segundo con el 22,27%, Meade tercero con el 16,40% y el independiente Jaime Rodríguez Calderón cuarto con el 5,23%. El morenista había sido el más votado en todos los estados salvo Guanajuato, decantado por Anaya. El 8 de agosto, por ultimo, el TEPJF dictaminó la validez de todo el proceso electoral y entregó la constancia de mayoría al ganador, haciéndose oficial su condición de presidente electo.\nLos resultados de las elecciones al Congreso de la Unión y de las celebradas en la mayoría de los estados terminaron de dibujar el alcance de la victoria de MORENA, comparable a un tsunami, y la derrota del PRI, calificable de colapso. El próximo Gobierno de López Obrador iba a gozar de mayoría absoluta en los dos hemiciclos federales: en la Cámara de Diputados, los tres partidos de Juntos Haremos Historia sumaban 308 escaños de 500 y en el Senado 69 escaños de 128. De las nueve gubernaturas sometidas a renovación el 1 de julio, MORENA se llevó las de la Ciudad de México (para Claudia Sheinbaum), Chiapas (Rutilio Escandón), Tabasco (Adán Augusto López) y Veracruz (Cuitláhuac García), mientras que su aliado el PES (Cuauhtémoc Blanco) se hizo con la de Morelos. El PAN, por su parte, retuvo los ejecutivos de Guanajuato y Puebla, y arrebató al PRI el de Yucatán, lo mismo que el Movimiento Ciudadano el de Jalisco. En los comicios a los congresos de 27 estados, MORENA fue la fuerza más votada 18, quedo segunda en siete, tercera en uno y cuarta en otro.\n(Cobertura informativa hasta 6/8/2018).\nMás información:\nWeb de Andrés Manuel López Obrador\nAndrés Manuel López Obrador en X\nAndrés Manuel López Obrador en Facebook\nAndrés Manuel López Obrador en YouTube\nAndrés Manuel López Obrador en Instagram\nAndrés Manuel López Obrador en Telegram\nAndrés Manuel López Obrador en TitTok\nAndrés Manuel López Obrador en Spotify\nPresidente de México\nPortal del Gobierno de México\nMovimiento Regeneración Nacional (MORENA)\nCIDOB is an international affairs research centre that, through excellence and relevance, seeks to analyse the global issues that affect political, social and governance dynamics, from the international to the local.\nPie de página\nAbout CIDOB\nResearch\nPublications\nNews\nEvents", "excerpt_sha256": "dc60b0790be8b782a91b198ad4896835349c98116152af8a3110fb3c613570a2", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0026-E014", "source_id": "SRC-26432e3d86aa471d", "url": "https://freedomhouse.org/country/mexico/freedom-net/2023", "title": "Mexico: Freedom on the Net 2023 Country Report", "publisher": "Freedom House", "raw_sha256": "55052483d1e8d22f7617e18dfe2b38b2b0aba2027783df80646eae8053c9bdea", "fact_summary": "During the reporting period, López Obrador's administration left IFT and COFECE without functioning plenaries by not filling vacancies; after a Supreme Court order, López Obrador appointed three COFECE commissioners. The Federal Economic Competition Commission (COFECE) is another regulatory body that, in 2021, was recognized by the Federal Judiciary (PJF) as the competent authority to regulate the markets for online search engines, social networking, and cloud computing services.\n2\nHowever, under López Obrador, IFT and other independent bodies have become regular targets of government pressure that has included sizable budget reductions.\n3\nLópez Obrador’s administration has also hindered regulatory bodies with bureaucratic delays. After the president declined to fill vacancies on the boards of IFT and COFECE in recent years, leaving them both without a functioning plenary, the bodies appealed to the Supreme Court of Justice of the Nation (SCJN).\n4\nIn November 2022, the SCJN ruled in favor of COFECE and ordered the executive branch to appoint commissioners;\n5\nLópez Obrador complied by appointing three new commissioners.\n6\nThe IFT’s case remained pending by the end of the coverage period, and civil society organizations have demanded a prompt response from the SCJN.\n7\nThe president’s failure to name commissioners to the autonomous bodies has been considered detrimental to the institutions' independence and ability to function properly.\n8\nPreviously, a 2020 government proposal to merge IFT with two other regulators was indefinitely postponed after it was condemned by civil society for threatening the regulator’s independence.\n9\nIn January 2021, President López Obrador announced a planned reform to incorporate IFT and the National Institute for Transparency, Access to Information, and Personal Data Protection (INAI) into the federal government, removing their autonomy.\n10\nThough the president later decided against dissolving IFT, instead delaying any action until the next administration,\n11\nhe reiterated his support for disbanding INAI in April 2023 (see C6).\n12\nDespite receiving some criticism in recent years for its decisions on antitrust measures and draft net neutrality guidelines,\n\n13\nIFT has continued to reaffirm its independence. In May 2021, for instance, IFT challenged the creation of a biometric cell phone registry—one that Congress had directed the agency to create, operate, and maintain—as unconstitutional (see C4).\n14\nFrom 2019 to 2021, IFT was criticized for its Traffic Management and Internet Administration Guidelines, which civil society actors claimed were an attempt to undermined net neutrality in Mexico (see B6).\n15\nNew guidelines went into effect in September 2021.\n16\nIn 2017, IFT was also criticized for authorizing América Móvil’s Telcel to exploit 60 megahertz of the 2.5 gigahertz spectrum band.\n17\nAssociations including the Telecommunications Law Institute (IDET) and the Competitive Intelligence Unit (CIU) argued that this move in fact reinforced the dominant player, contradicting IFT’s mandate to guarantee competition and equal conditions for all agents in the sector.\n18\n1\nJuan Montes, “Mexico Telecoms Reform Bill Advances,” The Wall Street Journal, March 22, 2013,\nhttp://on.wsj.com/1LXSc6E\n2\n“El Poder Judicial de la Federación resuelve que la COFECE es la autoridad competente para conocer de los mercados de servicios de búsqueda en línea, redes sociales y de cómputo en la nube [The Federal Judiciary ruled that COFECE is the competent authority to review the markets for online search services, social networks and cloud computing],” COFECE, June 18, 2021,\nhttps://www.cofece.mx/el-poder-judicial-de-la-federacion-resuelve-que-l…\n3\nYared de la Rosa, “En 3 años, gobierno redujo presupuesto de Inai, IFT, Cofece, CNH y CRE hasta 77% [In 3 years, the government reduced the budget of Inai, IFT, Cofece, CNH and CRE to 77%],” Forbes México, January 11, 2021,\nhttps://www.forbes.com.mx/politica-3-anos-gobierno-presupuesto-inai-ift…\n.\n4\nJorge Monroy, \"Cofece acude a la SCJN para destrabar nombramientos [Cofece goes to the SCJN to unblock appointments]”, El Economista, December 10, 2021,\nhttps://www.eleconomista.com.mx/empresas/La-Cofece-presenta-controversi…\n; Despite announcing a temporary pause on operations in February 2022, the outlet returned in July of that year to again “review the relationship between the press and power in Puebla.”\n3\nAmapola, an outlet from Guerrero, questions the state’s narrative regarding criminal violence.\n4\nSustained efforts to create outlets that represent diverse experiences have also found success in Mexico. Homosensual is one of the most widely read LGBT+ websites in Latin America and was nominated for outstanding Spanish-language online journalism in the Gay & Lesbian Alliance Against Defamation (GLAAD) 2021 Media Awards.\n5\nAs public awareness about online manipulation and the spread of disinformation has increased, fact-checking and data journalism initiatives have sought to counter false information presented through official channels and other media. As of December 2022, for instance, SPIN Taller de Comunicación Política alleged that President López Obrador made at least 101,155 false, misleading, or unprovable claims in the first 1,484 days of his term after fact-checking his daily press conferences.\n6\n1\n“Los medios digitales nativos pagan un precio por perseguir la verdad” [Native digital media pay a price for chasing the truth], Sembramedia, accessed January 6, 2020,\nhttp://data.sembramedia.org/vulnerabilidad/?lang=es\n.\n2\nFor example: Based in Mexico City, Pie de Página is an initiative born from the experience of the Periodistas de a pie network and Radios Libres, a project that seeks to boost communitarian radios with free technologies. Another innovative initiative in the digital media landscape is Pictoline, born at the end of 2015. In April 2022, during the previous coverage period, feminist protests were held across the country after 18-year-old Debanhi Escobar, who had gone missing earlier that month, was found dead in Monterrey; protesters called for justice in the Escobar case and for other missing women. The protests were sparked by an image of Escobar taken on the night of her disappearance, which circulated on social media.\n6\nEscobar’s parents sought to mobilize support and demand justice for their daughter via digital platforms; in June 2022, they publicly called on supporters to post an image of a lit candle on social media with the hashtag #JusticiaparaDebanhi (#JusticeForDebanhi).\n7\nActivism for internet freedom issues also occurs in Mexico. Article 190 requires providers to maintain records of their users’ metadata for a period of two years, and grants security agencies access to these records at any time.\n1\nIn 2016, the SCJN upheld the constitutionality of the law’s requirements for data retention and real-time geolocation. However, the ruling established the need for a judicial warrant to access historical metadata.\n2\nBeginning in March 2023 and through the end of the coverage period, the INAI, Mexico’s data protection authority and access to information body, was unable to function properly due to the lack of five-person quorum of commissioners. Despite a federal judge ordering the Senate to appoint a commissioner to at least one of the INAI’s three vacant seats,\n3\nthe Senate failed to do so at the end of May, after MORENA legislators accused the judiciary of interfering in its affairs.\n4\nAs of June, nearly 6,000 cases were awaiting resolution by the INAI.\n5\nAmidst the deadlock in May, Human Rights Watch called on the government to appoint commissioners, emphasizing that the INAI’s inability to function left Mexicans vulnerable to potential abuses of their personal data.\n6\nThe Senate’s failure to appoint the INAI commissioners comes amid calls from López Obrador and his allies to dissolve the body entirely and broader efforts by the president to undermine independent regulatory bodies (see A5).\n7\nIn August 2023, after the coverage period, the second chamber of the SCJN ruled that the INAI could meet with only four commissioners until a five-member quorum is appointed, allowing it to begin processing outstanding cases.\n8\nReforms to the criminal procedural code in 2016 required a judicial warrant for government entities to access geolocation data, with some exceptions, such as kidnapping cases in which a person’s life or physical integrity is in danger.\n9\nThe 2016 SCJN ruling also clarified which authorities can access user data; it included federal prosecutors, federal police, and the agency directly in charge of applying and coordinating the National Security Law.\nAn April 2021 reform of the Federal Telecommunications and Broadcasting Law established a biometric cell phone registry that tasked telecommunications companies with collecting biometric data; the SCJN ultimately ruled the registry unconstitutional in April 2022 (see C4).\n10\nThe database would have been accessible to authorities overseeing public security and justice.\n11\n\nDuring the coverage period, Congress again moved to create a biometric database that could undermine the right to privacy. A new bill that the Senate will examine, which was approved by the Chamber of Deputies in March 2023, would empower the Ministry of the Interior (SEGOB) to create and manage a centralized biometric database, the National Registry and Identity System (SID).\n12\nThe SID would require individuals to give biometric information to the SEGOB while registering activities at the Civil Registry, meaning that citizens would effectively have no means to consent to the collection of their personal biometric data.\n13\nCivil society organizations and some opposition lawmakers have raised concerns that the bill does not clearly specify which biometric data would be collected and which entities would have access to it, posing a risk to privacy and the protection of personal data.\n14\n1\nSee Articles Mexico remains the most dangerous country in Latin America for journalists and one of the most dangerous in the world.\n1\nArticle 19 documented 696 aggressions against journalists in 2022,\n2\nmaking it the most violent year for journalists since the organization started tracking in 2007. These figures include 181 instances of intimidation and harassment, 151 threats, 48 physical attacks, and 12 murders of journalists in potential relation to their work. Of all reported attacks against the press, 196 (28.19 percent) occurred in the digital sphere, including online intimidation and harassment, digital threats, and attempts to hack journalists' accounts.\n3\nNearly 49 percent of the total attacks against journalists related to coverage of corruption and politics, while 27.59 percent were linked to security and justice issues.\n4\nThe Committee to Protect Journalists (CPJ) separately reported that 13 journalists, including digital reporters,\n5\nwere killed in 2022, making it the deadliest year on record for journalists in Mexico.\n6", "excerpt_sha256": "ab20fc63ab6d9902748506cbd6a00d7515d8a6aa4e5bb3f971c0fed70f429c76", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0029-E003", "source_id": "SRC-07d1ccd98af71f80", "url": "https://www.animalpolitico.com/politica/amlo-varado-carretera-acapulco-huracan-otis", "title": "AMLO Delayed on Road to Acapulco After Otis", "publisher": "Animal Político", "raw_sha256": "03127e1d7867c90298049a5497b4b049033e1b3496d0535049695f00c01bf2c2", "fact_summary": "After Hurricane Otis made landfall as a Category 5 storm on October 25, 2023, AMLO traveled toward Acapulco but was delayed for more than six hours by mud, landslides, blocked roads, and failed vehicle passage; sin embargo decidió tomar la carretera México-Acapulco y su camioneta quedó atascada en lodo.\nPero además, a las 9 de la noche, en Presidencia informaron que López Obrador estaría a la misma hora de siempre ―7 de la mañana― encabezando la\nconferencia de prensa mañanera y antes estaría con el gabinete de seguridad\n.
